Management 
(JUDGMENT) 
(Passed on this 7 day of October - 2025) 


As per letter dated 0/0/2020 by the Government of India, Ministry of Labour, New Delhi, the reference is 
made to this Tribunal under Section-l0 of Industrial Disputes Act, l947 (in short the ‘Act’)as per Notification 
No. J-(4-2)/2020-IR dt. 0/0/2020. The dispute under reference relates to: 


“Whether the action of the management of M/s.PrimeOne Workforce Pvt. Ltd. and M/s.Kaapro 
Management Solutions Pvt. Ltd. in terminating the services of workman Shri Mukund Sharma working in 
the establishment of Registrar, Indian Institute of Technology, Indore (M.P.) w.e.f. 76.02.2078 and 
47.02.2076 respectively, are just & proper? If yes, what relief the workman concerned is entitled to and 
from which date?” 


Thereafter, corrigendum dated 22.07.2022 was received containing amended reference which is as follows: 


"Whether the action of the management of the Registrar. Indian Institute of Technology(IIT), Indore 
(M.P.)/ M/s.PrimeOne Pvt. Ltd. and M/s. Kaapro Management Solutions Pvt. Ltd. in terminating the services of 


7696 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 3, 2026/PAUSHA 3, 947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


workman Shri Mukund Sharma working in the establishment of Registrar. Indian Institute of Technology, Indore 
(M.P.) w.e.f. 76.02.2078 and 77.02.2076 respectively, are just and proper? If yes, what relief the workman 
concerned is entitled to and from which date?" 


Notices were issued to both the parties. They appeared and filed their respective statement of claim and 
defense. 


The workman appeared and filed his statement of claim with copy of his appointment order, pay slip, 
dismissal order. 


IIT also appeared through their counsel. But did not file any written statement of defense. 


The Outsourcing Agency M/s Kaapro Industries and M/s Prime One Workforce did not appear inspite 
of service of notice; hence reference proceeded ex-parte against them. 


In evidence, the workman filed his affidavit as his examination-in-chief. None appeared for cross- 
examination. He also filed photocopy of his appointment letter, pay slip and dismissal order. 


I have heard argument of Learned Counsel for workman Mr. Pranay Choubey. None appeared for 
Management. I have gone through the record as well. 


The amended reference is the issue for determination in the case in hand. 


Case of the Workman as taken by him in his statement of claim is mainly that, he was appointed by IIT as a 
Driver on !9.05.20 at consolidated salary and continued to work as a Driver till date of dismissal of his services 
which is 5.02.208. It is then, he came to known that his services have been shown through outsourcing agencies 
M/s Prime One Workforce which is incorrect on facts. In fact, he was appointed by IIT and continued to work for IIT 
as their employee. The Contractor or Outsourcing agency was simply a camouflage to deprive him the admissible 
benefits under law. He has further alleged that, his termination is bad in law and has requested his reinstatement as an 
employee of IIT with back wages and benefits. 


In his affidavit he has corroborated his allegations. 


His appointment letter dated !9.05.20, photocopy on record, filed by him, shows that it was for this month 
only and at consolidated pay of Rs. 0,000/- per month and for six months. Thus it was a fixed term appointment. 
There is nothing on record to show that this service contract was extended by IIT. The case of the Workman is further 
that, he was shown to be an outsourced employee, which is a camouflage as required to be proved by him, in which 
he has failed. 


The established facts that emerge are that, after his contract with IIT came to end, he would have been 
engaged through Outsourcing Agencies. Hence, there is no employee-employer relationship between him and IIT at 
the time he was disengaged by the Outsourcing Agency M/s Prime One. Hence, IIT cannot be fastened with any 
liability to reinstate or to compensate him. 


Since, his statement that he has worked for 240 days in a year and since 206, in which he has been shown 
as outsourced employee has been uncontroverted, hence he is held to have been proved his continuous engagement 
with the outsourcing agency M/s Prime One for more than 240 days at least in the year preceding his disengagement 
since his case that no notice or compensation was paid to him on his termination, his termination is held in violation 
of Section 25 F and 25G of the Act. 


As regards to the relief, keeping in view his tenure of engagement with the outsourcing agency M/s 
Prime One, he is held entitled to a lump sum compensation of Rs. 25,000/- in lieu of all his claims payable to 
him within 30 days from the date of publication of Award, failing which interest @6% per annum till payment. 


Reference stands answered accordingly. 
DATE:- 7/0/2025 
P.K. SRIVASTAVA, Presiding Officer 


नई दिल्‍ली, 24 दिसम्बर, 2025

SALONI, Dy. Director

595, Dr. Ambedkar Colony,

Workman 
Versus 
The General Manager 
Ordnance Factory, Khamaria, 
Jabalpur - 482005 
Management 
AWARD 


(Passed on this 2" day of September-2025.) 


As per letter dated 0/09/202 by the Government of India, Ministry of Labour, New Delhi, the reference is 
made to this Tribunal under इस्टलांगा-0 of I.D. Act, 947 (in short the ‘Act’) as per reference number 
L-40/7/202/IR(DU) dt. 0/09/202. The dispute under reference related to :- 


“Whether the demand raised by Fire Brigade Karmachari Sangh, Jabalpur (M.P.) against the 
management of Ordnance Factory, Khamaria, Jabalpur to give 40 hours duty instead of 48 hours in a 
week and to give 5 days duty instead of 06 days to fire brigade section employees as per Ordnance Factory 
Board Letter No. 525/D/A/A(Pt)/V dated 73.05.7986, is proper, legal and justified ? If yes, to what relief 
the fire brigade section employees are entitled to ? What other directions, if any, are necessary in 
the matter ?” 


After registering the case on the basis of the reference received, notices were sent to the parties and were 
duly served on them. They appeared and filed their respective statements of claim and defense. 


In short, the case of the workman union is that the members of the union who are Fire Brigade Employees 
work with the management in their factory, the Defense Ordnance Factory Board issued a Circular No. 
525/D/A/A(pt)/V dated 3.05.988 for fixation of duty hours for grant of overtime allowances to four categories of its 
employees namely viz; Durbans, Fire Brigade Staff, Telephone Operators and Security Assistant, which provided that 
the these workmen shall qualify for overtime allowances for the work done above 44.I5 hours which was earlier 48 
hours a week. Case of the workman union is further that the management is not deputing them on work for a period 
more than 44.I5 hours in a week just to deny them the benefits of overtime allowances, which is unjust, illegal and 
arbitrary. 


Workman union has further prayed that the management be directed to depute the Fireman for 44 hours or 
more in a week of 6 days instead of 40 hours a week which is at present. 


[भाग II—ave 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 3, 2026/पौष 3, 947 7699 


Management did not appear and did not file any written statement of defense. 
The workman union filed affidavit of its Secretary and also the photocopy of the Circular mentioned above. 


I have heard argument of learned Counsel Mr. Arun Patel for the workman union. Management never 
participated in this case. I have gone through the record as well. 


The only issue of determination is whether the management can be obligated in law to depute the fireman 
on duty for more than 44.5 hours or 48 hours and more in a week, so that they may be entitled to overtime 
allowances. 


Settled principle is that working hours of a workman is fixed. If management employs the workman for 
working hours more than fixed as maximum, it is required to pay overtime for the additional hours, the workman has 
been on duty. If management takes work from its employee for a period less than what is fixed, there is no law to 
compel the management to take work from the workman for a period more than the workman is required to work, just 
to enable to workman to claim overtime that too when there is no provision that the employer is bound to employ the 
workman for a minimum period of hours on work. 


In the light of above discussion, the claim of the workman union fails and the reference is answered as 

follows :- 
AWARD 

Holding the demand raised by Fire Brigade Karmachari Sangh, Jabalpur (M.P.) against the 
management of Ordnance Factory, Khamaria, Jabalpur to give 40 hours duty instead of 48 hours in a week 
and to give 5 days duty instead of 06 days to fire brigade section employees as per Ordnance Factory Board 
Letter No. 525/D/A/A/A(Pt)//V dated 3.05.988, improper, illegal and unjustified, the fire brigade section 
employees are entitled to no relief. 


No order as to cost. 
DATE: 2/09/2025 
P.K. SRIVASTAVA, Presiding Officer 


नई दिल्‍ली, 24 दिसम्बर, 2025

सरकार भारतीय भूवैज्ञानिक सर्वेक्षण, कोयला ड्रिलिंग प्रभाग, कोलकाता के निदेशक (ड्रिलिंग) और श्री महेश विश्वकर्मा,

7700 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 3, 2026/PAUSHA 3, 947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


Versues 
The Director (Drilling), 
Geological Survey of India, 
Coal Drilling Division, 
D.K.6, Sector, 
Salt Lake City, Kolkata - 70009 


Management 


(JUDGMENT) 
(Passed on this 067 day of October - 2025) 


As per letter dated 28/0/209 by the Government of India, Ministry of Labour, New Delhi, the reference is 
made to this Tribunal under Section-l0 of Industrial Disputes Act, l947 (in short the ‘Act’)as per Notification 
No. L-4202/203/208-(IR(DU)) dt. 28/0/209. The dispute under reference relates to: 


“Whether the Geological Survey of India is an Industry under the ID Act 7947, if so, whether the action 
of the Management of Geological Survey of India in terminating the services of Shri Mahesh 
Viswakarma, Drilling Helper #.०९.-३०/०४/2078 who was working on daily rated basis w.e.f. 04/02/7968 and 
completed more than 240 days in every Calendar year is fair and legal? If not, to what relief the workman 
is entitled to?” 


Notices were issued to both the parties. They appeared and filed their respective statement of claim and 
defense. 


Case of the Workman is mainly that, he was initially appointed as Drilling Helper by the Management vide 
its order dated 04.02.988 on daily rate basis, was posted under Unit In-charge Drilling Camp, Lalpur Distt. Shahdol 
and worked till 30.04.208 continuously for more than 240 days in every year. The Establishment is an ‘industry’ and 
an ‘industrial establishment’ is defined under the Act. The applicant is a ‘Workman’ as defined under the Act. The 
Act of termination of the Workman by Management is ‘retrenchment’ under the Act. According to the Workman, his 
termination by Management in spite of his continuous working from 04.02.988 to 30.04.20i8 without any notice or 
compensation is in violation of Section 25F and 25G of the Act. Also, it has been alleged that, the same category of 
the Workman engaged by Management after engagement of the Applicant Workman have been retained in service, 
hence the Management has acted in violation of Section 76 and 77 of the Industrial Disputes (Central) Rules, !956 (in 
short the ‘Rules’) in prohibiting the principle first come last go. Further, it has been alleged that the Management has 
adopted unfair practice by engaging the Workman as contract worker for 30 years which is prohibited under the Act. 
The Workman has thus prayed that holding the action of Management against law, the Workman be held entitled to 
be reinstated with back wages and consequential benefits. 


In its written statement of defense filed by the Management, they have pleaded that they are not an 
‘Industry’ as defined under Section 2(j) of the Act. The applicant is not a workman under the Act hence the reference 
is not cognizable by this Tribunal. Further, they have pleaded that, the applicant was never issued any appointment or 
posting letter at any point of time. He was not appointed by Management as Drilling helper vide order dated 
04.02.988 as claimed by him nor was he subsequently posted under Unit 482 as he claims. His engagement was 
perpetual temporary in nature for intermittent period on contract and on as and when required basis. The Management 
has further denied that no Workman junior to the Applicant Workman have been retained and has requested that 
reference be answered against the Applicant Workman. 


In its rejoinder the Workman has mainly reiterated his case as taken by him in his statement of claim. 


In evidence, the Workman has filed and proved sanction letter of post, two certificates with respect to 
engagement, work sanction orders for the period 203, 204, 205, internal communications with respect to payment 
of muster roll of skilled drilling helper and other unskilled contract labour for the years 2007, 2008, 2009, 20I0, 20I4, 
20i2, 20i3, 20i4, Certified statements of Accounts of Workman, marked as Ex. W-| to W-5 respectively. The 
Workman has further filed his affidavit as his examination in chief. He has been cross-examined by Management. 


The Management has filed affidavit of its witness Arobindo Biswas as his examination-in-chief. He has been 
cross-examined by Workman. The Management has filed photocopy pre-receipt bills not admitted by Workman. 
Management has not proved these photocopy documents. 


I have heard arguments of Learned Counsel for the Workman Mr. K.B. Singh and Mr. S.K. Mishra 
Learned Counsel for the Management. Workman has filed written submission also. I have gone through the written 
submissions and record. 


[भाग [Ive 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 3, 2026/पौष 3, 947 770 


On perusal of record in the light of rival submission following issues arise for determination — 


l. Whether the Management is an ‘Industry’ under the Act and applicant is a Workman as defined under 
the Act? 


2. Whether the disengagement of the Workman by Management is in violation of the Act? 


Whether the Management has adopted unfair labour practice by engaging a contract Worker for a period 
from 79868 to 20762 


4. Relief if any to which the Workman may be entitled to? 
Issue No.  — 
Before proceeding, certain provisions require to be reproduced which are as follows: 


Section 2(s) — workman means any person (including an apprentice) employed in any industry to do any 
manual, unskilled, skilled, technical, operational, clerical or supervisory work for hire or reward, whether the 
terms of employment be express or implied, and for the purposes of any proceeding under this Act in relation 
to an industrial dispute, includes any such person who has been dismissed, discharged or retrenched in 
connection with, or as a consequence of, that dispute, or whose dismissal, discharge or retrenchment has led 
to that dispute, but does not include any such person— 


(i) who is subject to the Air Force Act, 7950 (45 of 7950), or the Army Act, 7950 (46 ०7950), or the 
Navy Act, 7957 (62 ०7957), or 


(ii) who is employed in the police service or as an officer or other employee of a prison; or 
(iii) who is employed mainly in a managerial or administrative capacity; or 
(iv) who, being employed in a supervisory capacity, draws wages exceeding ten thousand rupees per 


mensem or exercises, either by the nature of the duties attached to the office or by reason of the 
powers vested in him, functions mainly of a managerial nature. 


Section 2(j) - Industry means any business, trade, undertaking, manufacture or calling of employers and 
includes any calling, service, employment, handicraft, or industrial occupation or avocation of workmen; 


It has been submitted from the side of the Management that, they are not an industry as defined under the 
Act. Judgment of Hon’ble High Court of Andhra in the Case of Geological Survey of India Employees Association 
Vs. Regional Labour Commissioner (Central) and other W.P. No. ३23/977 decided by single bench on 9.06.978 
has been referred to in this respect. The Single bench of Hon’ble High Court has held that the Geological Survey of 
India is not an Industry as defined under the Act. The relevant portion of the judgment is being reproduced as follows 


“TI do not consider it necessary to consider the question whether the Geological Survey of India exercises 
sovereign functions, as I am of the view that this is a case governed by separate rules and constitutional provisions. 
As has been already observed, its employees are governed by the Civil Service Regulations, & Fundamental rules 
governing Government employees and these provisions would, in the words of Justice Krishna Iyer "remove from 
the scope of the Act categories which otherwise may be covered thereby" (Vide IV(d) at page 596, Volume 2 of the 
report.” 


It has been submitted from the side of the Workman that firstly, this Judgment is not binding on this 
Tribunal, rather it is only a possessive value and secondly, this judgment is against the statutory provisions. 


The bare reading of the Section 2(j) of the Act shows that Industry is a systematic activity, carried by 
cooperation by Employer and its workman for production, supply or distribution of goods or services with a view to 
satisfy human wants or wishes. It also makes it clear that, purpose of the activity to make profit or investment of 
capital is not material in this respect. In the leading case of Bangalore Water and Sewage Board V.s. A. Rajappa 
AIR 978 Supreme Court 548 corresponding citation (978) 2 SCC 23. This judgment has been referred by the 
Single Bench also; the relevant portions of the judgment are being reproduced as follows: 


“440, “Industry’, as defined in Section 2(j) and explained in Banerji, has a wide import. 


“(a) Where (i) systematic activity, (ii) organized by co-operation between employer and employee (the direct 
and substantial element is chimerical) (iii) for the production and/or distribution of goods and _ services 
calculated to satisfy human wants and wishes (not spiritual or religious but inclusive of material things or 
services geared to celestial bliss e.g. making, on a large scale prasad or food), prima facie, there is an ‘industry’ 
in that enterprise. 


(b) Absence of profit motive or gainful objective is irrelevant, be the venture in the public, joint, private or 
other sector. 


(c) The true focus is functional and the decisive test is the nature of the activity with special emphasis on the 
employer-employee relations. 


7702 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 3, 2026/PAUSHA 3, 947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


(d) If the organization is a trade or business it does not cease to be one because of philanthropy animating 
the undertaking.” 


447. Although Section 2(j) uses words of the widest amplitude in its two limbs, their meaning cannot be 
magnified to overreach itself. 


“(a) ‘Undertaking’ must suffer a contextual and associational shrinkage as explained in Banerji and in this 
judgment; so also, service, calling and the like. This yields the inference that all organized activity possessing the 
triple elements in I, although not trade or business, may still be ‘industry’ provided the nature of the activity, viz. 
the employer-employee basis, bears resemblance to what we find in trade or business. This takes into the fold of 
‘industry’ undertakings, callings and services, adventures ‘analogous to the carrying on the trade or business’. 
All features, other than the methodology of carrying on the activity viz. in organizing the co-operation between 
employer and employee, may be dissimilar. It does not matter, if on the employment terms there is analogy.” 


442. Application of these guidelines should not stop short of their logical reach by invocation of creeds, cults or 
inner sense of incongruity or outer sense of motivation for or resultant of the economic operations. The ideology of 
the Act being industrial peace, regulation and resolution of industrial disputes between employer and workmen, the 
range off this statutory ideology must inform the reach of the statutory definition. Nothing less, nothing more. 


“(a) The consequences are (i) professions, (ii) clubs, (iii) educational institutions, (iv) co-operatives, (v) 
research institutes, (vi) charitable projects, and (vii) other kindred adventures, if they fulfil the triple tests listed 
in I, cannot be exempted from the scope of Section 2(j). 


(b) A restricted category of professions, clubs, co-operatives and even gurukulas and little research 
labs, may qualify for exemption if, in simple ventures, substantially and, going by the dominant nature criterion, 
substantively, no employees are entertained but in minimal matters, marginal employees are hired without 
destroying the non-employee character of the unit. 


(c) If, in a pious or altruistic mission many employ themselves, free or for small honoraria or like 
return, mainly drawn by sharing in the purpose or cause, such as lawyers volunteering to run a free legal 
services clinic or doctors serving in their spare hours in a free medical centre or ashramites working at the 
bidding of the holiness, divinity or like central personality, and the services are supplied free or at nominal cost 
and those who serve are not engaged for remuneration or on the basis of master and servant relationship, then, 
the institution is not an industry even if stray servants, manual or technical, are hired. Such eleemosynary or like 
undertakings alone are exempt — not other generosity, compassion, developmental passion or project.” 


43, 7#€ dominant nature test: 


“(a) Where a complex of activities, some of which qualify for exemption, others not, involves employees on 
the total undertaking, some of whom are not ‘workmen’ as in the University of Delhi case [University of 
Delhi v. Ramlfath, (/964) 2 SCR 703 : AIR 7963 SC 7873 : (7963) 2 Lab LJ 335] or some departments are not 
productive of goods and services if isolated, even then, the predominant nature of the services and the integrated 
nature of the departments as explained in the Corporation of Nagpur will be the true test. The whole undertaking 
will be ‘industry’ although those who are not ‘workmen’ by definition may not benefit by the status. 


(b) Notwithstanding the previous clauses, sovereign functions, strictly understood, (alone) qualify for 
exemption, not the welfare activities or economic adventures undertaken by government or statutory bodies. 


(c) Even in departments discharging sovereign functions, if there are units which are industries and they are 
substantially severable, then they can be considered to come within Section 2()). 


(d) Constitutional and competently enacted legislative provisions may well remove from the scope of the Act 
categories which otherwise may be covered thereby.” 


3574. Thus, in order to draw the “circle of industry”, to use the expression of my learned Brother Iyer, we do not 
find even the term “workman” illuminating. The definition only enables us to see that certain classes of persons 
employed in the service of the State are excluded from the purview of industrial dispute which the Act seeks to provide 
for in the interests of industrial peace and harmony between the employers and employees so that the welfare of the 
nation is secured. The result is that we have then to turn to the preamble to find the object of the Act itself, to the 
legislative history of the Act, and to the socio-economic ethos and aspirations and needs of the times in which the Act 
was passed. 


42, The method which has been followed, whether it be called interpretation or construction of a part of an 
organic whole in which the statute, its objectives, its past and its direction for the future, its constitutional setting are 
all parts of this whole with their co related functions. Perhaps it is impossible, in adopting such a method of 
interpretation, which some may still consider unorthodox, a certain degree of subjectivity. But, our attempt should be 
not to break with the well established principles of interpretation in doing so. Progressive, rational and beneficial 


[भाग [IVS 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 3, 2026/पौष 3, 947 7703 


modes of interpretation import and fit into the body of the old what may be new. It is a process of adaptation for 
giving new vitality in keeping with the progress of thought in our times. All this, however, is not really novel, although 
we may try to say it in a new way. 


453. If one keeps in mind what was laid down in Heydon case referred to by my learned Brother Krishna Iyer, the 
well-known principle that a statute must be interpreted as a whole, in the context of all the provisions of the statute, 
its objects, the preamble and the functions of various provisions, the true meaning may emerge. It may not be strictly 
a dictionary meaning in such cases. Indeed, even in a modern statute the meaning of a term such as “industry” may 
change with a rapidly changed social and economic structure. For this proposition I can do no better than to quote 
Subba Rao, J. speaking for this Court in Senior Electric Inspector v. Laxmi Narayan Chopra [AIR 7962 SC 59 : 
(2962) 3 SCR l46] : (SCR pp. /56-5/7) 


“The legal position may be summarized thus: The maxim contem-poranea exposito as laid down by Coke 
was applied to construing ancient statutes but not to interpreting Acts which are comparatively modern. There is 
a good reason for this change in the mode of interpretation. The fundamental rule of construction is the same 
whether the Court is asked to construe a provision of an ancient statute or that of a modern one, namely, what is 
the expressed intention of the legislature. It is perhaps difficult to attribute to a legislative body functioning in a 
Static society that its intention was couched in terms of considerable breadth so as to take within its sweep the 
future developments comprehended by the phraseology used. It is more reasonable to confine its intention only to 
the circumstances obtaining at the time the law was made. But in a modern progressive society it would be 
unreasonable to confine the intention of a legislature to the meaning attributable to the word used at the time the 
law was made, for a modern legislature making laws to govern a society which is fast moving must be presumed 
to be aware of an enlarged meaning the same concept might attract with the march of time and with the 
revolutionary changes brought about in social, economic, political and scientific and other fields of human 
activity. Indeed, unless a contrary intention appears, an interpretation should be given to the words used to take 
in new facts and situations, if the words are capable of comprehending them.” 


367. The test indicated above would necessarily exclude the type of services which are rendered purely for the 
satisfaction of spiritual or psychological urges of persons rendering those services. These cannot be bought or sold. 
For persons rendering such services there may be no “industry”, but, for persons who want to benefit from the 
services rendered, it could become an “industry”. When services are rendered by groups of charitable individuals to 
themselves or others out of missionary zeal and purely charitable motives, there would hardly be any need to invoke 
the provisions of the Industrial Disputes Act to protect them. Such is not the type of persons who will raise such a 
dispute as workmen or employees whatever they may be doing. 


462, This leads one on to consider another kind of test. It is that, wherever an industrial dispute could arise 
between either employers and their workmen or between workmen and workmen, it should be considered an area 
within the sphere of “industry” but not otherwise. In other words, the nature of the activity will be determined by the 
conditions which give rise to the likelihood of occurrence of such disputes and their actual occurrence in the sphere. 
This may be a pragmatic test. For example, a lawyer or a solicitor could not raise a dispute with his litigants in 
general on the footing that they were his employers. Nor could doctors raise disputes with their patients on such a 
footing. Again, the personal character of the relationship between a doctor and his assistant and a lawyer and his 
clerk may be of such a kind that it requires complete confidence and harmony in the productive activity in which they 
may be co-operating so that, unless the operations of the solicitor or the lawyer or the doctor take an organised and 
systematised form of a business or trade, employing a number of persons, in which disputes could arise between 
employers and their employees, they would not enter the field of industry. The same type of activity may have both 
industrial and non-industrial aspects or sectors. 


463, I would also like to make a few observations about the so-called “sovereign” functions which have been 
placed outside the field of industry. I do not feel happy about the use of the term “sovereign” here. I think that the 
term ‘sovereign’ should be reserved, technically and more correctly, for the sphere of ultimate decisions. Sovereignty 
operates on a sovereign plane of its own as I suggested in Keshavananda Bharati case (973) 4 SCC 225] supported 
by a quotation from Ernest Barker's Social and Political Theory. Again, the term “Regal”, from which the term 
“sovereign” functions appears to be derived, seems to be a misfit in a Republic where the citizen shares the political 
sovereignty in which he has even a legal share, however small, inasmuch as he exercises the right to vote. What is 
meant by the use of the term “sovereign”, in relation to the activities of the State, is more accurately brought out by 
using the term “governmental” functions although there are difficulties here also inasmuch as the Government has 
entered largely new fields of industry. Therefore, only those services which are governed by separate rules and 
constitutional provisions, such as Articles उ709 and उा47 should, strictly speaking, be excluded from the sphere of 
industry by necessary implication. ”’ 


Nowhere in the Constituent Bench judgment, it has been laid down that the Rules by which an Employee is 
governed will be a factor to determine whether the establishment is an industry or not. Reference of Section 438 of 


7704 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 3, 2026/PAUSHA 3, 947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


the Industrial Disputes Standing Order Act, 946 is also required which is being reproduced as follows:- 


2३-28. Act not to apply to certain industrial establishments.--Nothing in this Act shall apply to an industrial 
establishment in so far as the workmen employed therein are persons to whom the Fundamental and 
Supplementary Rules, Civil Services (Classification, Control and Appeal)Rules, Civil Services (Temporary Service) 
Rules, Revised Leave Rules, Civil Service Regulations, Civilians in Defense Service (Classification, Control and 
Appeal) Rules or the Indian Railway Establishment Code or any other rules or regulations than may be notified in 
this behalf by the appropriate Government in the Official Gazette, apply. 


A bare perusal of these provisions make it clear that, even if the Establishment is governed by Fundamental 
and Subsidiary Rules, Civil Services Classification Control and Appeals Rules, Civil Services Temporary Services 
Rules, Civil Services Regulation or India Railways Establishment code or other regulations, may be an Industry as 
defined under the Act. It also provides that in case the Establishment or its employees as governed by these Codes 
and Rules, the provisions of the Industrial Employment Standing Orders Act, 946 will not apply. 


The aforesaid analysis makes it clear that, only because the establishment is govern by CCA Rules of 
Fundamental and Subsidiary Rules, it does not ceases to be an Industry under Section 2(j) of the Act if it is otherwise 
covered under this Section. 


In the light of above discussion, the referred Single Bench Judgment will not help the Management. 


Management has further referred to a judgment of a coordinate Bench of this Tribunal in Bangalore wherein 
the Geological Survey of India has been held not an ‘industry.’ But, this Tribunal has held this organization as 
‘industry’ in Case R/35/200. 


In the light of above discussion and findings, the Geological Survey of India is held an industry as 
defined under the Act. Consequently, the Applicant is held to be a Workman under the Act, and the dispute is 
held to be an Industrial Dispute under the Act cognizable by this Tribunal. 


Issue No.I is answered accordingly. 


Issue No. 2 — 


Two facts are to be considered while considering this issue. First, is whether the Workman has successfully 
proved his continuous engagement with the Management as defined under Section 25B of the Act for 240 days and 
year including the year preceding the date of his disengagement and secondly, whether his disengagement is in 
violation of Section 25F and 25G of the Act. 


As regards to the first point, Section 25B of the Act is being reproduced as follows:- 
25B. Definition of continuous service. - 
For the purposes of this Chapter — 


(J) a workman shall be said to be in continuous service for a period if he is, for that period, in uninterrupted 
service, including service which may be interrupted on account of sickness or authorised leave or an 
accident or a strike which is not illegal, or a lock-out or a cessation of work which is not due to any fault on 
the part of the workman; 


(2) where a workman is not in continuous service within the meaning of clause (/) for a period of one year or 
six months, he shall be deemed to be in continuous service under an employer— 


(a) for a period of one year, if the workman, during a period of twelve calendar months preceding the 
date with reference to which calculation is to be made, has actually worked under the employer for 
not less than— 


(i) one hundred and ninety days in the case of a workman employed below ground in a mine; 
and 
(ii) two hundred and forty days, in any other case; 
(b) for a period of six months, if the workman, during a period of six calendar months preceding the 


date with reference to which calculation is to be made, has actually worked under the employer for 
not less than— 


(i) ninety-five days, in the case of a workman employed below ground in a mine; and 
(ii) one hundred and twenty days, in any other case. 
Explanation 97 the purposes of clause (2), the number of days on which a workman has actually 


worked under an employer shall include the days on which— 


[भाग IVS 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 3, 2026/07 3, 947 7705 


(i) he has been laid-off under an agreement or as permitted by standing orders made under the 
Industrial Employment (Standing Orders) Act, /946 (20 of 7946), or under this Act or under any 
other law applicable to the industrial establishment; 


(ii) he has been on leave with full wages, earned in the previous years; 


(iii) he has been absent due to temporary disablement caused by accident arising out of and in the 
course of his employment; and 


(iv) in the case of a female, she has been on maternity leave; so, however, that the total period of such 
maternity leave does not exceed twelve weeks. 


Case of the Workman is that, he was first engaged as a Daily Wager on 04.02.998 and continued till 
30.04.208 i.e. the date of his disengagement. 


In his affidavit, as his examination in chief he has corroborated this allegation, and has stated that he had 
continuously worked for 30 years ie; from 04.02.998 and continued till 30.04.20I8 & more than 240 days in every 
year. In his cross-examination, he has stated that, he was not issued a formal appointment letter, he denied that he was 
engaged by a Contractor or a labour contractor. Regarding the bills and vouchers photocopy produced by the 
Management, he has stated that these documents do not bear his signatures. 


On the other hand, case of the Management is that, he was a daily wager engaged on as and when required 
basis. Management witness has stated that he was not appointed after any recruitment process. No appointment letter 
was issued to him. He was engaged on contractual basis for intermittent periods and his contractual employment came 
to end after closure of the Drilling Camp. He never worked continuously for 240 days in any year. 


This witness has been confronted in his cross-examination with respect to Ex. W-l and W-2 filed and 
proved by Workman. W-I! is sanction letter dated 2.03.988 for engagement of casual labour with a list of the 
persons employed as casual labour containing the name of the applicant workman and W-2? is the sanction orders for 
the period June, 20i3 to August, 20l4, showing the name of the workman in the list of payments of labour 
contractors. This witness has stated that, he cannot tell about these documents. When confronted with Ex. W-3 and 
W-4 filed and proved by the Workman Ex. W-4 is the muster roll of skilled and unskilled workers total l8 in number 
for the period from 2007 to 20I4, prepared by Management and W-5 the certified statement of the account of the 
Workman, this witness has stated that he cannot tell about these documents also. 


It comes out that, firstly, the Management takes a case that the Workman was employed as a Casual Labour 
on as and when required basis and secondly, the documents specially Ex. W-2 mention that he was a Labour 
Contractor. These documents are the documents of the Management, there is not on record, any document filed and 
proved by Management with regard to contract labour or supply of contract labour by a labour contractor has been 
filed. Hence, the case of Management that, the Workman was labour contractor is not reliable and is doubtful. From 
the documents and statements as referred above, the case of the Workman that he was engaged by the Management 
since 04.02.988 till 30.04.20I4 is held proved. It is also held proved that he has worked continuously with 
Management for the period stated above as defined under Section 25B of the Act. 


As regards to the second point, the Management witness has admitted that, no notice or compensation was 
given to the Workman on his disengagement. Case of Management is that the project was closed, hence the workmen 
were disengaged but there is no document corroborating this fact of closure. On the other hand, case of the workman 
is that the Work is still going on. Hence, in the light of the these facts, the disengagement of the Workman by 
Management is held in violation of Section 25F and 25G of the Act. These provisions are being reproduced as 
follows.... 


25F. Conditions precedent to retrenchment of workmen.— No workman employed in any industry who has been 


in continuous service for not less than one year under an employer shall be retrenched by that employer until— 


(a) the workman has been given one month’s notice in writing indicating the reasons for retrenchment 
and the period of notice has expired, or the workman has been paid in lieu of such notice, wages for 
the period of the notice; 


(b) the workman has been paid, at the time of retrenchment, compensation which shall be equivalent to 
fifteen days' average pay for every completed year of continuous service or any part thereof in 
excess of six months; and 


(c) notice in the prescribed manner is served on the appropriate Government or such authority as may 
be specified by the appropriate Government by notification in the Official Gazette. 


25G. Procedure for retrenchment.— 


Where any workman in an industrial establishment, who is a citizen of India, is to be retrenched and he 
belongs to a particular category of workmen in that establishment, in the absence of any agreement between the 


7706 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 3, 2026/PAUSHA 3, 947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


employer and the workman in this behalf, the employer shall ordinarily retrench the workman who was the last 
person to be employed in that category, unless for reasons to be recorded the employer retrenches any other 
workman. 


On the basis of above discussion Issue No. 2 is answered accordingly. 
Issue No. 3 — 


For the sake of the convenience, Section 2(r)(a), 25T and 25U as well the 57 Schedule of the Act (relevant 
provisions) are being reproduced as follows:- 


(ra) “unfair labour practice” means any of the practices specified in the Fifth Schedule; 


25T. Prohibition of unfair labour practice — No employer or workman or a trade union, whether registered under 
the Trade Unions Act, 926 (8 of 926), or not, shall commit any unfair labour practice. 


25U. Penalty for committing unfair labour practices— Any person who commits any unfair labour practice shall 
be punishable with imprisonment for a term which may extend to six months or with fine which may extend to one 
thousand rupees or with both. 


THE FIFTH SCHEDULE 

[See section 2(ra)] 

UNFAIR LABOUR PRACTICES 

I.—On the part of employers and trade unions of employers 


(0). To employ workmen as “badlis”, casuals or temporaries and to continue them as such for years, with the object 
of depriving them of the status and privileges of permanent workmen. 


From the above discussion it is held proved the Management had engaged the Workman as casual labour 
from 988 to 208. This is an unfair labour practice under 57 Schedule mentioned above. 


The Act prohibits unfair labour practice and provides punishment for such practice. The Act is silent with 
respect to Civil consequences of unfair labour practice. 


Since, unfair labour practice on the part of the Management is prohibited under the Act, the Courts and 
Tribunals are within their powers to undo the effect of such unfair labour practices that were adopted by the 
Management. 


The following paragraphs of the judgment of Hon’ble Supreme Court in the case of Dharam Singh & Ors 
VsState Of U.P. &Anr Civil Appeal No. 8558 of 20I8 are being reproduced in this respect as follows : 


#| जला public institutions depend, day after day, on the same hands to perform permanent tasks, equity demands 
that those tasks are placed on sanctioned posts, and those workers are treated with fairness and dignity. The 
controversy before us is not about rewarding irregular employment. It is about whether years of ad hoc engagement, 
defended by shifting excuses and pleas of financial strain, can be used to deny the rights of those who have kept 
public institutions running. We resolve it by insisting that public employment should be organised with fairness 
,reasoned decision making, and respect for the dignity of work. 


l. Furthermore, it must be clarified that the reliance placed by the High Court on Umadevi (Supra) to non suit the 
appellants is misplaced. Unlike Umadevi(Supra), the challenge before us is not an invitation to bypass the 
constitutional scheme of public employment. It is a challenge to the State’s arbitrary refusals to sanction posts despite 
the employer’s own acknowledgement of need and decades of continuous 


reliance on the very workforce. On the other hand, Umadevi (Supra) draws a distinction between illegal 
appointments and irregular engagements and does not endorse the perpetuation of precarious employment where the 
work itself is permanent and the State has 


failed, for years, to put its house in order. Recent decisions of this Court in Jaggo v. Union of India 2024 SCC OnLine 
SC 3826 and in Shripal & Another v. Nagar Nigam, 2025 SCC OnLine SC 22].Ghaziabad have emphatically 
cautioned that Umadevi (Supra) cannot be deployed as a shield to justify exploitation through long-term “ad hocism”, 
the use of outsourcing as a proxy, or the denial of basic Civil Appeal No. 8558 of 208 9parity where identical duties 
are exacted over extended periods. The principles articulated therein apply wilfull force to the present case. The 
relevant paras from Shripal (supra) have been reproduced hereunder: The Respondent Employer places reliance on 
Umadevi (supra) to contend that daily-wage or temporary employees cannot claim permanent absorption in the 
absence of statutory rules providing such absorption. However, as frequently reiterated, Uma Devi itself distinguishes 


[भाग [Ive 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 3, 2026/07 3, 947 7707 


between appointments that are “illegal” and those that are 


“irregular,” the latter being eligible for regularization if they meet certain conditions. More importantly, Uma Devi 
cannot serve as a shield to justify exploitative engagements persisting for years without the Employer undertaking 
legitimate recruitment. Given the record which shows no true contractor-based arrangement and a consistent 


need for permanent horticultural staff the alleged asserted ban on fresh recruitment, though real, cannot justify 
indefinite daily-wage status or continued unfair practices. 


5. It is manifest that the Appellant Workmen continuously rendered their services over several years, sometimes 
spanning more than a decade. Even if certain muster rolls were not produced in full, the Employer's failure to furnish 
such records despite directions to do so-allows an adverse inference under well-established labour jurisprudence. 
Indian labour law strongly disfavors perpetual daily-wage or contractual engagements in circumstances where the 
work is permanent in nature. Morally and legally, workers who fulfill ongoing municipal requirements year after year 
cannot be dismissed summarily as dispensable, particularly in the absence of a genuine contractor agreement. At this 
juncture, it would be appropriate to recall the broader critique of indefinite “temporary” employment practices as done 
by a recent judgment of this court in Jaggo v. Union of India in the following paragraphs: - 


“22. The pervasive misuse of temporary employment contracts, as exemplified in this case, reflects a broader 
systemic issue that adversely affects workers’ rights and job security. In the private sector, the rise of the gig economy 
has led to an increase in precarious employment arrangements, often Civil Appeal No. 8558 of 208 characterized by 
lack of benefits, job security, and fair treatment. Such practices have been criticized for exploiting workers and 
undermining labour standards. Government institutions, entrusted with upholding the 


principles of fairness and justice, bear an even greater responsibility to avoid such exploitative employment practices. 
When public sector entities engage in misuse of temporary contracts, it not only mirrors the detrimental trends 
observed in the gig economy but also sets a concerning precedent that can erode public trust in governmental 
operations. 


25. It is a disconcerting reality that temporary employees, particularly in government institutions, often face 
multifaceted forms of exploitation. While the foundational purpose of temporary contracts may have been to address 
short-term or seasonal needs, they have increasingly become a mechanism to evade long-term obligations owed to 
employees. These practices manifest in several ways:- 


Misuse of “Temporary” Labels: - Employees engaged for work that is essential, recurring, and integral to the 
functioning of an institution are often labelled as “temporary” or “contractual,” even when their roles mirror those of 
regular employees. Such misclassification deprives workers of the dignity, security, and benefits that regular 
employees are entitled to, despite performing identical tasks. 


eArbitrary Termination: - Temporary employees are frequently dismissed without cause or notice, as seen in the 
present case. This practice undermines the principles of natural justice and subjects workers to a state of constant 
insecurity, regardless of the quality or duration of their service. 


¢ Lack of Career Progression: Temporary employees often find themselves excluded from opportunities for skill 
development, promotions, or incremental pay raises. They remain stagnant in their roles, creating a systemic disparity 
between Civil Appeal No. 8558 of 208 them and their regular counterparts, despite their contributions being equally 
significant. 

¢ Using Outsourcing as a Shield: - Institutions increasingly resort to outsourcing roles performed by temporary 
employees, effectively replacing one set of exploited workers with another. This practice not only perpetuates 
exploitation but also demonstrates a deliberate effort 


to bypass the obligation to offer regular employment. 


¢ Denial of Basic Rights and Benefits: - Temporary employees are often denied fundamental benefits such as 
pension, provident fund, health insurance, and paid leave, even when their tenure spans decades. This lack of social 
security subjects them and their families to undue hardship, especially in cases of illness, retirement, or unforeseen 
circumstances.” 


As we have observed in both Jaggo (Supra) and Shripal (Supra), outsourcing cannot become a convenient 
shield to perpetuate precariousness and to sidestep fair engagement practices where the work is inherently perennial. 
The Commission’s further contention that the appellants are not “full-time” employees but continue only by virtue of 
interim orders also does not advance their case. That interim protection was granted precisely because of the long 
history of engagement and the pendency of the challenge to the State’s refusals. It neither creates rights that did not 
exist nor erases entitlements that may arise upon a proper adjudication of the legality of those refusals. 


Before concluding, we think it necessary to recall that the State (here referring to both the Union and the 
State governments) is not a mere market participant but a constitutional employer. It cannot balance budgets on the 
backs of those who perform the most basic and recurring public functions. Where work recurs day after day and year 
after year, the establishment must reflect that reality in its sanctioned strength and engagement practices. The long- 


7708 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 3, 2026/PAUSHA 3, 947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


term extraction of regular labour under temporary labels corrodes confidence in public administration and offends the 
promise of equal protection. Financial stringency certainly has a place in public policy, but it is not a talisman that 
overrides fairness, reason and the duty to organise work on lawful lines. 


8. Moreover, it must necessarily be noted that “ad-hocism” thrives where administration is opaque. The 
State Departments must keep and produce accurate establishment registers, muster rolls and outsourcing 
arrangements, and they must explain, with evidence, why they prefer precarious engagement over sanctioned posts 
where the work is perennial. If “constraint” is invoked, the record should show what alternatives were considered, 
why similarly placed workers were treated differently, and how the chosen course aligns with Articles [4, 6 and 2] 
of the Constitution of India. Sensitivity to the human consequences of prolonged insecurity is not sentimentality. It is 
a constitutional discipline that should inform every decision affecting 


those who keep public offices running. 


9. Having regard to the long, undisputed service of the appellants, the admitted perennial nature of their duties, and 
the material indicating vacancies and comparator regularisations, we issue the following 


directions: - 


i. Regularization and creation of Supernumerary posts: All appellants shall stand regularized with effect from the 
date on which the High Court directed a fresh recommendation by the Commission and a fresh decision by the State 
on sanctioning posts for the appellants. For this purpose, the State and the successor establishment (U.P. Education 
Services Selection Commission) shall create supernumerary 


posts in the corresponding cadres, Class-III (Driver or equivalent) and Class-IV (Peon/Attendant/Guard or equivalent) 
without any caveats or preconditions. On regularization, each appellant shall be placed at not 


less than the minimum of the regular pay-scale for the post, with protection of last-drawn wages if higher and the 
appellants shall be entitled to the subsequent increments in the pay scale as per the pay grade. For seniority and 
promotion, service shall count from the date of regularization as given above. 


ii. Financial consequences and arrears: - Each appellant 


shall be paid as arrears the full difference between (a) the pay and admissible allowances at the minimum of the 
regular pay-level for the post from time to time, and 


(b) the amounts actually paid, for the period from 24.04.2002 until the date of regularization /retirement/death, as the 
case may be. Amounts 


already paid under previous interim directions shall be so adjusted. The net arrears shall be released within three 
months and if in default, the unpaid amount shall carry compound interest at 6% per annum from 


the date of default until payment. 


iii. Retired appellants: - Any appellant who has already retired shall be granted regularization with effect from 
24.04.2002 until the date of superannuation for pay fixation, arrears under clause (ii), and recalculation of pension, 
gratuity and other terminal dues. The revised 


pension and terminal dues shall be paid within three months of this Judgement. 


iv. Deceased appellants: - In the case of Appellant No. 5 and any other appellant who has died during pendency, 
his/her legal representatives on record shall be paid the arrears under clause (ii) up to the date of death, together with 
all terminal/retiral dues recalculated consistently with clause (i), within three months of this Judgement. 


These propositions of law and observations fully apply in the case in hand. 
In the light of above discussion and findings, the reference is answered as follows. 
AWARD 


The action of the Management of Geological Survey of India in terminating the services of Shri Mahesh 
Viswakarma, Drilling Helper #॥.९३०/०४/2078 who was working on daily rated basis w.e.f. 0९4/02//988 and 
completed more than 240 days in every Calendar year is held unfair and illegal. 


The workman shall be reinstated by Management Bank with back wages and all consequential benefits. 
He shall stand regularized with effect from the date on which he first raised the dispute before the Conciliation 
Officer. He shall be paid as arrears, the full difference between the pay and admissible allowances at the minimum 
of the regular pay-level for the post from time to time, and the amounts actually paid, for the period until the date 
of regularization, as the case may be. The arrears shall be released within 30 days from the date of publication of 
Award. In case of default, the unpaid amount shall carry interest at 6% per annum from the date of Award until 
payment. 


[भाग [Ive 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 3, 2026/07 3, 947 7709 


The Workman shall also be paid litigation cost Rs. 25,000/- by Management within the time framed 
mentioned above.

(JUDGMENT) 


(Passed on this 2"" day of November, 2025) 


As per letter dated 30/09/20I5 by the Government of India, Ministry of Labour, New Delhi, the reference 
has been made to this Tribunal under Section-0 of Industrial Disputes Act, 7947 (in short the ‘Act’) as per 
Notification No. 7-72072/96/2075 (IR(B-I)) dt. 30/09/20I5. The dispute under reference relates to:- 


[भाग II—ave 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 3, 2026/पौष 3, 947 77i] 


" क्‍या वैनिक वेतनभोगी बैंक कर्मचारी संगठन TANT At देवेन्द्र Ms को विनांक 9..2005 स्रे विनांक 02.06.2073 
तक भारतीय स्टेट बैंक भ्रोपात्र की शाखा नई आबावी मंदसौर में काम करना बताकर 20 प्रतिशत बोनस राशि की मांग करना 
ATTRA हैं? अगर हां तो at वेवेन्द्र गॉड किस अनुतोष के अधिकारी हैं" 


After registering the case on the basis of reference, notices were issued to the parties. They appeared and 
filed their respective statement of claim in defense. 


According to the workman union the claimant Devendra Goud was appointed against the vacant post of 
Peon by the then Branch Manager, Naiabadi Branch, Mandasaur on 9..2005 and worked 8 hours a day regularly 
till 02.06.203, when he was disengaged by the Bank without any notice or compensation. He had worked 
continuously for 240 days in every year and had discharged the duties of Peon for which he was paid his wages. He 
was paid bonus @ 8.33% whereas he was entitled to bonus @ 20% which is being paid by the other Banks. 
According to the union, this action of management is unjust, illegal and arbitrary. 


The union has requested that holding the action of management is unjust, illegal and arbitrary. He be held 
entitled to bonus @ 20% 


According to the management Bank, the claimant was engaged on daily wages purely on temporary basis 
in the branch on, as and when required basis. He worked purely on temporary basis intermittently when there was 
requirement, he worked 40 days in the year 2005-06, 20 days in 2007-08, 35 days in 2008-09, 79 days in 2009-0, 
44 days in 200-Il, 32 days in 20Ii-2 and 2 days in 203-4, for which he was paid his wages and bonus. The 
management Bank has denied the claim of the workman union that the workman worked continuously for a period of 
240 days and more in every calendar year. 


In evidence, the workman union has filed affidavit of the workman Devendra Goud as his Examination-in- 
chief, he has been cross-examined by management Bank. 


He filed and proved 7#-५७/।, Application before Labour Commissioner with respect to payment of bonus, 
Ex-W/2, sheet showing details of payment of bonus from the year 2005 to 20I3-l4, Banker cheque with respect to 
bonus, circulars with respect to minimum wages issued in different years from 2005-203, certificate issued by the 
Branch Manager on 4.05.20l] and note photocopy with respect to workers allotted and payment to the Bank for 
cleaning and certain vouchers with respect to payment, letters issued by the Branch Manager on |7.0.20I3 and 
03.09.203, to the Treasury Officer authorizing the workman to receive stamps issued by treasury, around 50 payment 
vouchers with respect to payment for cleaning from !.07.2009 to 20I, which are marked as Ex-W/] to Ex-W/25. 
The workman had filed Application seeking production of documents from management Bank. The Bank has filed 
copies of payment register for the period 03.04.200 to 06.07.203 with respect to payments regarding cleaning of the 
Branch to the claimant workman and other persons total 29 pages. 


Management has filed affidavit of its witness Mr. Sumit Gupta, he has been cross-examined by workman 
union. 


I have heard argument of Learned Counsel for workman Mr. Arun Patel and Mr. Praveen Yadav, Learned 
Counsel for management Bank. 


The reference itself is the issue for determination in the case in hand. 


The minimum bonus which a management is required to pay under section l0 of Bonus Act, is 8.33%. For, 
payment of bonus at higher rate, there has to be an agreement between the workman and management. No such 
agreement has been filed. Hence, action of management Bank in not paying bonus to the applicant workman @ 20% 
cannot be held to be unjustified. 


Hence, holding the demand of workman union for payment of bonus to the applicant workman Mr. 
Devendra Balram Gaud, @ 20%, unjustified, the reference stands answered accordingly.

(JUDGMENT) 


(Passed on this 2' day of November, 2025) 


As per letter dated 29/09/20I5 by the Government of India, Ministry of Labour, New Delhi, the reference 
has been made to this Tribunal under Section-0 of Industrial Disputes Act, 7947 (in short the ‘Act’) as per 
Notification No. 7-72077/56/2075077%5-7)) dt. 29/09/205. The dispute under reference relates to:- 


"क्या वैनिक वेतनभोगी बैंक कर्मचारी संघ TANT At aoa ATARTA MT CT (को विनाक 9.I.2005 से दिनाक 
62.06.2073 तक वैंनिक वेतन भोगी के रुप में काम करना बताने पर A देवेन्द्र बालाराम MS तक पुनरीक्षित वेतनमान पाने का 
हकवार हैं? यविहां तो oh ais वेतन में अंतर की कितनी राशि के हक़वार हैं?" 


After registering the case on the basis of reference, notices were issued to the parties. They appeared and 
filed their respective statement of claim in defense. 


According to the workman union the claimant Devendra Goud was appointed against the vacant post of 
Peon by the then Branch Manager, Naiabadi Branch, Mandasaur on 9..2005 and worked 8 hours a day regularly 
till 02.06.203, when he was disengaged by the Bank without any notice or compensation. He had worked 
continuously for 240 days in every year and had discharged the duties of Peon for which he was paid his wages. 


According to the union, this action of management is in violation of Section 25-F and 25-G of the Act, 
hence is unjust, illegal and arbitrary. 


The union has requested that holding the disengagement of the workman Devendra Goud, by the 
management Bank, against law, he be held entitled to be reinstated with all back wages and benefits. 


According to the management Bank, the claimant was engaged on daily wages purely on temporary basis 
in the branch on, as and when required basis. He worked purely on temporary basis intermittently when there was 
requirement, he worked 40 days in the year 2005-06, 20 days in 2007-08, 35 days in 2008-09, 79 days in 2009-0, 
44 days in 20i0-I, 32 days in 20I-2 and 2 days in 20i3-4, for which he was paid his wages. The management 
Bank has denied the claim of the workman union that the workman worked continuously for a period of 240 days and 
more in every calendar year. 


[भाग [I—ave 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 3, 2026/07 3, 947 773 


This is also the case of the management that the claimant workman was not appointed against any regular 
vacancy following recruitment process. He did not worked for 240 days in any year, hence, his disengagement by the 
Bank cannot be faulted in law. Management has requested that the reference be answered against the workman. 


In evidence, the workman union has filed affidavit of the workman Devendra Goud as his Examination-in- 
chief, he has been cross-examined by management Bank. 


He filed and proved 7#ऋ-५७/।, Application before Labour Commissioner with respect to payment of bonus, 
Ex-W/2, sheet showing details of payment of bonus from the year 2005 to 20I3-l4, Banker cheque with respect to 
bonus, circulars with respect to minimum wages issued in different years from 2005-203, certificate issued by the 
Branch Manager on 4.05.20l] and note photocopy with respect to workers allotted and payment to the Bank for 
cleaning and certain vouchers with respect to payment, letters issued by the Branch Manager on 7.0!.20l3 and 
03.09.203, to the Treasury Officer authorizing the workman to receive stamps issued by treasury, around 50 payment 
vouchers with respect to payment for cleaning from !.07.2009 to 20l, which are marked as Ex-W/I] to Ex-W/25. 
The workman had filed Application seeking production of documents from management Bank. The Bank has filed 
copies of payment register for the period 03.04.200 to 06.07.203 with respect to payments regarding cleaning of the 
Branch to the claimant workman and other persons total 29 pages. 


Management has filed affidavit of its witness Sumit Gupta, he has been cross-examined by workman union. 


I have heard argument of Learned Counsel for workman Mr. Arun Patel and Mr. Praveen Yadav, Learned 
Counsel for management Bank. 


The reference itself is the issue for determination in the case. 


The basis of claim of the workman with respect to regular and revised wages is the bipartite settlement. Case 
of the management bank is that the bipartite settlement does not apply to daily wagers rather it applies to the regular 
employees of the Bank. This is supported from the perusal of bipartite settlement. Hence, the claim of the workman 
union regarding revised wages to the applicant workman who is a daily wager casual employee is not admissible in 
law following judgments, cited by management bank are referred to in this respect. 


I. State of Haryana & Ors. v/s Tilak Raj & Ors., 2003 SC 2658 
2. Himanshu Kumar Vidyarthi & Ors. v/s State of Bihar & Ors., /997 SC 3657 
3. State of Rajasthan & Ors. v/s Dayalal & Ors., (2077) 2 SCC 429 


Hence, holding the claim of workman union with regard to revised wages to the applicant workman, 
unjustified, the reference stands answered accordingly.

Vs 

The Chief General Manager, 
State Bank of India, Zonal Office, 
L.H.O. Hoshangabad Road, 
Bhopal (M.P.)- व] 

Management 

(JUDGMENT) 


(Passed on this 2' day of November, 2025) 


As per letter dated 30/09/20I5 by the Government of India, Ministry of Labour, New Delhi, the reference 
has been made to this Tribunal under Section-l0 of Industrial Disputes Act, 7947 (in short the ‘Act’) as per 
Notification No. 7-72077/49/2075(077(8-7)) dt. 30/09/205. The dispute under reference relates to:- 


" क्या दैनिक वेतनभोगी कर्मचारी संघ दावरा श्री देवेन्द्र बबराम MS वैनिक वेतन भोगी (HET (ANAT स्टेट बैंक 
Ma AGAR को बैंक दवारा विनाक79.77.05 से त्रगायत प्रतिवर्ष 240 दिन से अधिक कार्य करने पर दिनाक 02.06.73 से बिना 
सूचना नोटिस पगार व्‌ छटनी मुआवजा fav बैंक से पृथक करने की मांग न्यायोचित हैँ. यदि हां तो at ats किस अनुतोष के 
भ्रागी a ” 


After registering the case on the basis of reference, notices were issued to the parties. They appeared and 
filed their respective statement of claim in defense. 


According to the workman union the claimant Devendra Goud was appointed against the vacant post of 
Peon by the then Branch Manager, Naiabadi Branch, Mandasaur on 9..2005 and worked 8 hours a day regularly 
till 02.06.203, when he was disengaged by the Bank without any notice or compensation. He had worked 
continuously for 240 days in every year and had discharged the duties of Peon for which he was paid his wages. 


According to the union, this action of management is in violation of Section 25-F and 25-G of the Act, 
hence is unjust, illegal and arbitrary. 


The union has requested that holding the disengagement of the workman Devendra Goud, by the 
management Bank, against law, he be held entitled to be reinstated with all back wages and benefits. 


According to the management Bank, the claimant was engaged on daily wages purely on temporary basis 
in the branch on, as and when required basis. He worked purely on temporary basis intermittently when there was 
requirement, he worked 40 days in the year 2005-06, 20 days in 2007-08, 35 days in 2008-09, 79 days in 2009-0, 
44 days in 20i0-I, 32 days in 20II-i2 and 2 days in 203-4, for which he was paid his wages. The management 
Bank has denied the claim of the workman union that the workman worked continuously for a period of 240 days and 
more in every calendar year. 


This is also the case of the management that the claimant workman was not appointed against any regular 
vacancy following recruitment process. He did not work for 240 days in any year, hence, his disengagement by the 
Bank cannot be faulted in law. Management has requested that the reference be answered against the workman. 


In evidence, the workman union has filed affidavit of the workman Devendra Goud as his Examination-in- 
chief, he has been cross-examined by management Bank. 


He filed and proved Ex-W/I, Application before Labour Commissioner with respect to payment of bonus, 
Ex-W/2, sheet showing details of payment of bonus from the year 2005 to 20I3-4, Banker cheque with respect to 
bonus, circulars with respect to minimum wages issued in different years from 2005-203, certificate issued by the 


[भाग [I—ave 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : जनवरी 3, 2026/पौष 3, 947 775 


Branch Manager on !4.05.20l] and note photocopy with respect to workers allotted and payment to the Bank for 
cleaning and certain vouchers with respect to payment, letters issued by the Branch Manager on !7.0.20I3 and 
03.09.203, to the Treasury Officer authorizing the workman to receive stamps issued by treasury, around 50 payment 
vouchers with respect to payment for cleaning from !.07.2009 to 20ll, which are marked as Ex-W/I] to Ex-W/25. 
The workman had filed Application seeking production of documents from management Bank. The Bank has filed 
copies of payment register for the period 03.04.200 to 06.07.203 with respect to payments regarding cleaning of the 
Branch to the claimant workman and other persons total 29 pages. 


Management has filed affidavit of its witness Sumit Gupta, he has been cross-examined by workman union. 


I have heard argument of Learned Counsel for workman Mr. Arun Patel and Mr. Praveen Yadav, Learned 
Counsel for management Bank. Both the sides have filed written arguments which are part of record. I have perused 
the written argument also gone through the record as well. 


On perusal of record in light of rival arguments, following Issues arises: - 


है Whether the workman union has successfully proved the engagement of the workman for 240 
days in a year including the year preceding the date of his termination as per section 25-B of 
the Act? 

2) Whether the disengagement of the workman by management is in violation of section 25-F and 
25-G of the Act? 

3) Subject to findings on issue No. I and 2, whether the workman is entitled to any relief? 

Issue No.|, 


Section 25-B of the Act, which defines ‘continuous service’, is being reproduced as follows:- 


“25B. Definition of continuous service.—For the purposes of this Chapter,— 


(7) a workman shall be said to be in continuous service for a period if he is, for that period, 
in uninterrupted service, including service which may be interrupted on account of 
sickness or authorised leave or an accident or a strike which is not illegal, or a lock-out 
or a cessation of work which is not due to any fault on the part of the workman; 


(2) where a workman is not in continuous service within the meaning of clause (2) for a 
period of one year or six months, he shall be deemed to be in continuous service under 
an employer— 


(a) for a period of one year, if the workman, during a period of twelve calendar 
months preceding the date with reference to which calculation is to be made, 
has actually worked under the employer for not less than— 


(i) one hundred and ninety days in the case of a workman employed below 
ground in a mine; and 


(ii) two hundred and forty days, in any other case; 


(b) for a period of six months, if the workman, during a period of six calendar 
months preceding the date with reference to which calculation is to be made, 
has actually worked under the employer for not less than— 


(i) ninety-five days, in the case of a workman employed below ground ina 
mine; and 
(ii) one hundred and twenty days, in any other case.” 


Pleadings of the parties on this issue have been detailed earlier. The workman has corroborated the allegation 
of the union on this issued. He has been cross-examined by management. He states that there was no advertisement 
released for the vacancy, no test conducted and also states that payments were made to him in different names. 


Learned Counsel for workman has submitted that he worked continuously with the bank for 240 days in 
every year and also in the year preceding the date of his termination i.e., 02.06.20I3 and this fact is established from 
the payment vouchers filed and proved by the workman, cash remittance register from November, 20!] to December, 
20] and from cash remittance register Naiabadi Branch from 03.0.20I] to 3.05.20i3 which have been filed by the 
Bank also in compliance of order of this Tribunal. 


Firstly, it is to be mentioned here that receiving payments by workman under different name is a crime, this 
is cheating by way of impersonation, hence, he cannot take advantage of his this statement. 


77\6 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 3, 2026/PAUSHA 3, 947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


On perusal of documents filed by him in support of his statement on oath, the certificate issued by the 
Branch Manager, does not specifically states that the workman has been continuously working. Secondly, Ex-20 
which is bunch of 48 photocopy documents, vouchers relating to Manish Telecom, Manish Computer, Stationery 
and VIP Communication shows that payments were made to the firm of Manish with respect to supply of stationery, 
etc. May be the payment was received by the workman, but it does not show that the workman worked as an 
employee of management Bank. The two documents which are authorization letter dated 7.0i.20i3 and 03.09.20I3 
authorizing the workman to receive stamp from treasury only indicates that the workman was engaged for getting 
stamp from treasury on these dates. Another bunch of documents is Ex-22 which are 45 photocopy receipts related to 
payment with respect to cleaning of the branch by the Bank to the claimant workman within the period ].07.2009 to 
200. Thus, bunch of document also indicates that the claimant was paid for dates mentioned in these documents in 
different months in different years. This also does not corroborate fully the case of the workman, worked for 240 days 
in any year. 


The payment details filed by the management Bank under order of this Tribunal, as mentioned above, 
contain the name of the workman as well other persons also, to whom the payments were made by the Bank. The case 
of the workman union is that payments were made to him by Bank under fictitious names will not be counted in his 
favour. Thus, these documents also do not fully corroborate the case of the workman on the point of his continuous 
engagement of 240 days or more in a year or year preceding the date of termination. It is the case of the workman that 
he was paid in different bogus names, this fact also goes against him. On the other hand, management witness has 
stated that the workman never worked for 240 days in any year, he has mentioned number of days in which he worked 
with the Bank in a year and that too for cleaning of the branch. There are photocopy vouchers filed by the workman 
union showing that water payments were made to the workman with respect to procurement of water cans which he 
received on behalf of the firm. Hence, the case laws referred to by the learned counsel for workman union in their 
written arguments do not support them. It is already the case of the management that the workman was engaged 
intermittently on as and when required basis. 


Hence, in light of this nature of evidence, the claim of the workman union that the applicant workman 
worked continuously for 240 days in any year or in a year preceding the date of disengagement is held not proved. 


Issue No. 4 is answered accordingly. 
Issue No. 2, 


In light of findings recorded on issue No. |, this issue is answered against the workman, thus his 
disengagement is justified in law. 


Issue No. 2 stands answered accordingly. 
Issue No. 3 
In light of aforesaid findings recorded on issue No. | and 2, the workman is entitled for no relief. 
Issue No. 3 stands answered accordingly 
In light of above discussion and findings, the reference is answered as follows.- 
AWARD 


“Holding the demand of Daily Wage Employees Union to separate Shri Devendra Balram Gaud, Daily 
Wage Employee (Peon), State Bank of India, Branch Mandsaur, from the bank with effect from 02.06.73 without 
notice, salary and retrenchment compensation, justified. Shri Devendra Balram Gaud is held entitled to no relief.” 


No order as to cost. 
DATE:- 2--2025 
P.K. SRIVASTAVA, Presiding Officer

THE CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL CUM LABOUR COURT, JABALPUR
